The warning directive `/*! msg */` is on the line before the reported line. So warnings are expected on the next line after the `/*! ... */` comment.

Now let me look at the `deprecatedComment` testdata positive to see examples. But more importantly, I need to design the new checker.

Let me think about what infrastructure is available:
- `ctx.Pkg` is the current `*types.Package`.
- `ctx.TypesInfo` has `Uses`, `Defs`, `Implicits`, `Scopes`, `Selections`, etc.
- `ctx.PkgObjects` maps `*types.PkgName` -> local name (requires `ctx.Require.PkgObjects = true`).
- For each file, we can use `f.Imports` directly.

To resolve a package reference:
- Given a package name (e.g., "fmt"), find the import spec in the current file where local name matches. For a normal import `import "fmt"`, local
name = package's actual name (from `types.PkgName`). For renamed import `import foo "fmt"`, local name = "foo". For dot import `import . "fmt"`, local
name is "." — not usable as `[pkg.X]`, but its symbols count as local for `[X]`.
- Need to map local name -> *types.Package. We can use `f.Imports` + `ctx.TypesInfo.Implicits`/`Uses`.

Let me think about how to get the imported package from a `*ast.ImportSpec`:
- For a named import `foo "fmt"`: `ctx.TypesInfo.ObjectOf(spec.Name)` returns `*types.PkgName` whose `.Imported()` is the package.
- For a default import `"fmt"`: `ctx.TypesInfo.Implicits[spec]` returns `*types.PkgName` whose `.Imported()` is the package.
- The local name: for named import, `spec.Name.Name`; for default import, `obj.Name()` (package's declared name, e.g. "fmt"). For dot import,
`spec.Name.Name` is "." and PkgName.Name is "."? Actually for dot imports, `obj.Name()` returns "."? Let me check. In go/types, `*types.PkgName` has
`Name()` which for dot import is "."? I think yes.

But there's an even simpler approach used by go/doc: `comment.Parser.LookupPackage` can be configured with a closure over a map from package name ->
import path. The `go/doc` package has `Package.LookupPackage` that uses the package's imports. But we need the actual `*types.Package` to look up
symbols, not just the import path. We could build a map from local package name -> *types.Package for the current file.

Let me design:

```go
type brokenDocLinkChecker struct {
    astwalk.WalkHandler
    ctx *linter.CheckerContext
}

func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) VisitDocComment(doc *ast.CommentGroup) {
    // but we need the declaration node position! DocCommentVisitor only passes the comment group.
}
```

The requirement says: "Emit each diagnostic at the position of the documented declaration node, not at the comment text itself."

The existing `DocCommentVisitor.VisitDocComment(*ast.CommentGroup)` only passes the comment group, not the declaration. So we need to extend the
walker or create a new visitor that passes the declaration node. The task explicitly says: "Extend the `astwalk` package with a `DocLinkVisitor`
interface and corresponding walker, following the pattern of existing visitors like `DocCommentVisitor`."

So we need to create:
```go
// DocLinkVisitor visits every doc-comment and its associated declaration.
type DocLinkVisitor interface {
    VisitDocLink(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)
}
```

Or perhaps `VisitDocComment(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)`. The task says "DocLinkVisitor" and "corresponding walker", so probably:

```go
type DocLinkVisitor interface {
    VisitDocLink(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)
}
```

And a `docLinkWalker` that mirrors `docCommentWalker` but also passes the declaration. Let's think about the walker logic. The `docCommentWalker`
visits:
- `*ast.FuncDecl`: decl.Doc
- `*ast.GenDecl`: decl.Doc
- `*ast.ImportSpec`: spec.Doc
- `*ast.ValueSpec`: spec.Doc
- `*ast.TypeSpec`: spec.Doc, and fields inside the type (field docs)

The new `DocLinkVisitor` should pass the declaration node whose doc comment contains the links. For a FuncDecl, the decl is the *ast.FuncDecl. For
GenDecl, the decl is the *ast.GenDecl (the documented declaration). For TypeSpec, the decl is the *ast.TypeSpec (or GenDecl?). For ValueSpec, the decl
is the *ast.ValueSpec. For field docs, the "declaration" is the *ast.Field.

But the requirement says "position of the documented declaration node". For a doc comment `// Foo ...` followed by `func Foo() {}`, the declaration
node is `*ast.FuncDecl`, and its position is at `func`. So we should use that node's position.

For `// T ...` followed by `type T struct {...}`, the doc comment is on the GenDecl (with decl.Doc), but the documented declaration node is the
TypeSpec T. Hmm. In Go AST, when you write:
```go
// T docs
type T struct{}
```
The `GenDecl` has `Doc` set to the comment, and `TypeSpec` has `Doc` nil (I think). But if you write:
```go
type (
    // T docs
    T struct{}
)
```
The `TypeSpec` has `Doc` set. So we need to handle both. The walker should pass the declaration node that the comment documents: for a GenDecl with
Doc, the "declaration" could be the GenDecl (position at `type` or `var`). But the requirement says "position of the documented declaration node".
Which node? Let's look at how the existing walker handles it. The `docCommentWalker` calls `VisitDocComment(decl.Doc)` for GenDecl and also for specs.
So for the pattern:
```go
// T docs
type T struct{}
```
it visits the GenDecl.Doc once. It does not also visit TypeSpec.Doc because TypeSpec.Doc is nil.

So the new walker should similarly visit each doc comment once, with the associated declaration node. For GenDecl.Doc, pass `decl` (the *ast.GenDecl).
For TypeSpec.Doc, pass the *ast.TypeSpec. For ValueSpec.Doc, pass *ast.ValueSpec. For FuncDecl.Doc, pass *ast.FuncDecl. For ImportSpec.Doc, pass
*ast.ImportSpec. For Field.Doc, pass *ast.Field.

But wait — for a GenDecl like:
```go
// T docs
type T struct{}
```
If we pass the GenDecl, its Pos() is at `type`, which is the line of the declaration. That satisfies "position of the documented declaration node".
For positive test directives, the warning should appear on the `type` line (the line after the doc comment). That's what the test format expects: `/*!
... */` comment before the line.

Actually, let me reconsider: "documented declaration node" for a `type T` probably means the `*ast.TypeSpec` (which starts at `T`), not the GenDecl
(`type` keyword). But the GenDecl is also the declaration node and its position is arguably the documented declaration. The task says "at the position
of the documented declaration node, not at the comment text itself" — the key point is just to not use the comment's position. Either the GenDecl or
TypeSpec position is fine as long as it's the declaration. But which one does the checker author likely intend? Let's think about how a user would
expect the warning to be attached: for `// docs` then `func Foo()`, attached at `func` line. For `type T struct{}`, attached at `type` line. Both
GenDecl.Pos and TypeSpec.Pos are on the same line typically. The test directive would be on the same line either way. So either works for tests as
long as the line matches.

I'll design the walker to pass the "outermost" declaration that owns the doc comment:
- For GenDecl with Doc: pass the GenDecl (position at `type`/`var`/`const` keyword).
- For TypeSpec with Doc: pass the TypeSpec (position at type name).
- For ValueSpec with Doc: pass the ValueSpec.
- For ImportSpec with Doc: pass ImportSpec.
- For Field with Doc: pass Field.
- For FuncDecl with Doc: pass FuncDecl.

But there's subtlety: for a GenDecl that has Doc, we should NOT also process spec.Doc if the spec.Doc is the same comment? In Go AST, when doc comment
is on GenDecl, spec.Doc is nil. When on spec, GenDecl.Doc is nil. So no double-visit. However, the current `docCommentWalker` always visits
GenDecl.Doc AND spec.Doc, which handles both cases. Fine.

Now the key question: how to validate links. Let me design the data structures.

First, I need to know which file's imports apply. Since the checker walks files one at a time (`WalkFile`), the walker needs to know the current file.
The `DocLinkVisitor` interface receives the declaration and doc comment, but to resolve imports we need the file. Options:
1. The walker can set the file on the visitor before visiting (like `EnterFile`).
2. The visitor can implement `EnterFile(*ast.File) bool` to capture the file.

The `WalkHandler` already provides `EnterFile`. The `docCommentWalker` currently doesn't call EnterFile (DocCommentVisitor doesn't include
walkerEvents). But the new `DocLinkVisitor` could include `walkerEvents` so the walker calls EnterFile and the visitor can store the current file. Or
simpler: the walker could pass the file as part of VisitDocLink. Let me check the task: "Extend the astwalk package with a DocLinkVisitor interface
and corresponding walker, following the pattern of existing visitors like DocCommentVisitor." It says "following the pattern of existing visitors like
DocCommentVisitor", which suggests a simple interface. But we need the file context.

Looking at how the `docCommentWalker` is structured, it just loops over decls and calls VisitDocComment. It doesn't call EnterFile. To get the file,
we can add the file to the visitor call: `VisitDocLink(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)` and also set a field on the checker through a custom
interface. Alternatively, the checker can implement its own `WalkFile` (not use astwalk), but the task explicitly says to use a DocLinkVisitor walker.

I think the cleanest: `DocLinkVisitor` interface:
```go
type DocLinkVisitor interface {
    walkerEvents
    VisitDocLink(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)
}
```
And the walker calls `w.visitor.EnterFile(f)` and `EnterFunc` etc. But the walker needs to maintain current file state to pass to VisitDocLink or the
visitor can capture via EnterFile. Since `WalkHandler.EnterFile` doesn't store the file, the checker struct can have a `file *ast.File` field and
override EnterFile. But then the checker has to implement EnterFile. That's fine.

Actually, simpler: the walker passes the `*ast.File` as a parameter:
```go
VisitDocLink(file *ast.File, decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)
```
But that's not "following the pattern of existing visitors". Hmm.

Let me look at how the existing `typeExprWalker` handles info - it stores `info` in the walker and passes it via WalkFile. But the visitor still
doesn't receive the file.

Let me look at how a checker gets the current file. `ctx.Filename` is set, but not the ast.File. Actually, `ctx.TypesInfo` and `ctx.Pkg` are set per
package, not per file. For imports we need per-file info.

Approach: the `DocLinkVisitor` includes `walkerEvents`, and the walker calls `EnterFile(f)` before traversing. The checker stores the file in its
struct via EnterFile. Then VisitDocLink uses `c.file`. This follows the pattern of e.g. CommentVisitor (which has walkerEvents) — actually
`CommentVisitor` includes `walkerEvents` too. Let me check comment_walker.go: it calls `w.visitor.EnterFile(f)`. So yes, `CommentVisitor` includes
walkerEvents. So `DocLinkVisitor` should include walkerEvents and the walker should call EnterFile. Good.

Now, let me look at how to resolve imports per file. I'll implement a helper that, given the current file, builds:
- `aliases map[string]*types.Package`: local name -> imported package (including dot imports? no, dot imports have no usable name, but their symbols
are added to package scope).
- `dotImports []*types.Package`: packages imported with `.`.

For package resolution:
- Given a package name `pkg` in a qualified reference `[pkg.Sym]`, look up `aliases[pkg]`. If found, use that package. If not found, report `package
"pkg" is not imported` (unless the reference has no symbol? e.g. `[pkg]` package-only also reports same).
- But wait: what about references using the full import path, like `[example.com/foo.Sym]`? Go doc supports `[pkg/path.Sym]`. The comment.Parser with
LookupPackage always-true would produce ImportPath="example.com/foo", Name="Sym". We can validate by finding an import whose path matches
`ImportPath`. That would be a nice feature but the task only mentions package names and renamed imports. It says "For qualified references, resolve
the package from the file's imports". Could include path-based. But message format says `"X" not found in package "pkg"` and `package "pkg" is not
imported`, where "pkg" is the package name as written (or alias). For a path-based reference, "pkg" would be the path. We should probably handle both:
if the reference contains "/", treat it as an import path and find matching import; else treat as local name/alias.

Let me focus on what the task explicitly requires:
- local references: look up in current package scope. Plus dot-imported symbols count as local.
- qualified references: resolve package from file imports, look up symbol in that package scope.
- renamed imports: use local alias as package name in messages.
- dot imports: dot-imported symbols count as local.
- builtins not flagged.
- non-type receiver: report `"F" is not a type`.
- method/field refs: verify both type and member exist, including members accessible through embedded fields.
- bracket content with spaces/non-identifier chars not treated as valid link.

Now, how to detect "bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is not treated as a valid link"? Given `comment.Parser` with
always-true LookupPackage, `[foo bar]` becomes DocLink{ImportPath:"foo bar"}. We can check: if the link text (as written) doesn't match a valid
symbol-link pattern, skip it.

But actually there's a subtlety: `comment.Parser` with LookupPackage always-true will call LookupPackage for `[foo bar]` and we'd return true. So the
DocLink is created. Then we need to skip because the text as written isn't a valid link. How to know "as written"? The DocLink has `Text []Text` which
contains the original bracket content as `[foo bar]` — in the test earlier, `DocLink.Text` showed `text=[foo bar]`. So we can reconstruct the link
text from `DocLink.Text` by concatenating the text parts (Plain, Italic). That gives us the raw content including spaces.

Alternatively, we can validate the fields:
- For a package-only link (`Name==""`): ImportPath must be a valid import path or valid identifier (no spaces, no invalid chars). `[foo bar]` →
ImportPath="foo bar" → invalid → skip.
- For a qualified link (`Name!=""`): ImportPath must be valid identifier/path; Recv if non-empty must be valid identifier; Name must be valid
identifier (parser already ensures Name is capitalized identifier via isName). But e.g. `[Foo.Bar Baz]` → ImportPath="Foo.Bar Baz" (contains
space/dot) → invalid → skip.
- `[foo-bar]` → ImportPath="foo-bar" → invalid → skip.

But wait — for `[foo-bar]`, the parser sets ImportPath="foo-bar", Name="". So we skip because "foo-bar" is not a valid identifier or import path
(contains hyphen; hyphen IS valid in import paths though! e.g. `github.com/some/pkg-name`). Hmm. Import paths can contain hyphens. So we can't reject
hyphens outright for path-based references. But `[foo-bar]` without a dot is a single package name; Go doc package links in bracket notation are only
valid for single-element stdlib package names like `[math]`, or package names resolved via imports. A hyphenated single-element name is not a valid Go
package import path or identifier. But `[foo-bar.Sym]` could be an import path `foo-bar.Sym`? No, package path with a hyphen like `foo-bar` as a
single element isn't a standard package name, but could be an alias? Actually import paths like `gopkg.in/yaml.v2` contain dots. `[foo-bar.Sym]` -
"foo-bar" could be an import path? It's a single element containing hyphen, which is allowed in import paths (e.g., module `rsc.io/quote`... no
hyphen). There are packages with hyphens in path elements, e.g. `github.com/go-critic/go-critic`... hyphen is allowed in path elements. But
`[foo-bar]` alone is not a full import path (no slash), and `foo-bar` isn't a valid package name either.

Let me not over-engineer. The requirement "Ensure bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is not treated as a valid link"
likely just means: when we see a DocLink whose raw text doesn't look like `ident`, `ident.ident`, `ident.ident.ident`, or a valid import path, we skip
it. We can reconstruct the raw text and validate it with a regex or by checking the parser's own `isName` semantics.

Actually, maybe a cleaner approach: don't set LookupPackage to always-true. Instead, set LookupPackage to a function that resolves the package name
against the file's imports (returning the import path) — so `[foo bar]` won't be a link (because "foo bar" isn't imported), and `[math]` won't be a
link unless math is imported. But then `[missingpkg.Foo]` where missingpkg isn't imported would NOT become a DocLink, so we couldn't report `package
"missingpkg" is not imported`. The task explicitly wants that message. So we DO need LookupPackage to always return true to get broken package
references as DocLinks. Then we filter invalid ones.

OK so plan:
1. `LookupPackage: func(name string) (string, bool) { return name, true }` — but this is called for all package candidates including invalid ones like
"foo bar". It returns (name, true), producing DocLink.
2. `LookupSym: func(recv, name string) bool { return true }` — produces DocLink for all local symbol/method candidates.
3. Walk the parsed Doc and collect DocLinks.
4. For each DocLink, reconstruct raw text (the bracket content as written) from `link.Text`.
5. Validate raw text:
   - Must match `^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$` (single symbol), `^ident\.ident$` (local method or qualified), `^ident\.ident\.ident$` (qualified method),
or package import path forms with dots/slashes. Simpler: split on ".", ensure no empty parts, each part must be a valid Go identifier OR (for the
first part when it's an import path) allow slashes and hyphens? Hmm.

Actually the comment.Parser already splits into ImportPath, Recv, Name. Let's think about which raw texts produce which fields:
- `[Foo]` → imp="", recv="", name="Foo"
- `[Foo.Bar]` → imp="", recv="Foo", name="Bar"
- `[pkg.Foo]` → imp="pkg", recv="", name="Foo"
- `[pkg.Recv.Method]` → imp="pkg", recv="Recv", name="Method"
- `[pkg]` → imp="pkg", recv="", name="" (where pkg is not capitalized; if capitalized e.g. `[Foo]`, it's treated as local symbol, not package)
- `[foo bar]` → imp="foo bar", recv="", name=""
- `[foo-bar]` → imp="foo-bar", recv="", name=""
- `[Foo.Bar Baz]` → imp="Foo.Bar Baz", recv="", name=""
- `[*Foo]` → imp="", recv="", name="Foo" (the `*` is trimmed by parser; raw text is `*Foo`)

So for validation:
- If Name != "": the `Name` is guaranteed a capitalized identifier by isName (for the dot cases). The `Recv` (if non-empty) comes from
`splitDocName(pkg)`, where pkg is before the last dot; for `[pkg.Foo]`, Recv is "". For `[pkg.Recv.Method]`, Recv="Recv". But Recv is also only set if
it's a capitalized identifier? Let's check: for `[pkg.lower.Method]`, splitDocName("pkg.lower") → i=3, name="lower" not capitalized → returns
("pkg.lower","",false); then pkg="pkg.lower", recv="". So Recv must be a capitalized identifier when set. Good.
- The `ImportPath` (when Name != "") is the part before the last dot. For `[pkg.Foo]`, imp="pkg". For `[pkg.Recv.Method]`, imp="pkg". This is the part
that may contain spaces/invalid chars (e.g. `[foo bar.X]` → splitDocName("foo bar.X") → i=7, name="X" cap, before="foo bar"; splitDocName("foo bar") →
not cap → pkg="foo bar", recv=""; so imp="foo bar"). So if imp contains spaces or invalid chars, skip.
- If Name == "": the whole text is ImportPath. For `[foo bar]`, imp="foo bar". For `[math]`, imp="math". For `[*Foo]`, Name="Foo" (so Name != "").

So we can validate the raw text. But maybe simpler: validate each DocLink by checking that its textual representation matches a valid pattern. Let me
reconstruct text from `link.Text`. In `comment.DocLink`, `Text []Text` is the original content inside brackets, e.g. `[Foo]` → Text=[Plain("Foo")];
`[*Foo]` → Text=[Plain("*Foo")]; `[foo bar]` → Text=[Plain("foo bar")]. We can join all text parts (Plain and Italic strings, plus any nested Link?
probably not) to get the raw content. Then:

```go
func isValidDocLink(text string) bool {
    // The link text as written must look like:
    //   ident
    //   ident.ident
    //   ident.ident.ident
    //   pkg/path (package import path)
    //   pkg/path.ident
    //   pkg/path.ident.ident
    // Reject anything containing spaces, or any non-identifier chars except allowed in import paths (slash, maybe hyphen).
}
```

Hmm, but how strict? Let's look at what Go's own `go/doc` does. Actually, in `go/doc`, the `LookupPackage` function is provided by the `doc.Package`
and uses the package's imports to resolve package names. It does NOT return true for arbitrary strings. So `[foo bar]` would simply not become a link
(LookupPackage("foo bar") returns false), and `[missingpkg.Foo]` also wouldn't become a link. So go/doc doesn't report broken links; the checker is
meant to be stricter.

Given the task's explicit message `package "pkg" is not imported`, they want `[missingpkg.Foo]` reported even when the package isn't imported. So we
need to capture all bracket candidates. The "not a valid link" filter is specifically to avoid false positives on things like `[foo bar]` that
obviously aren't meant to be links.

I think a reasonable, robust approach:
- Reconstruct the raw text from `link.Text`.
- Validate that the raw text matches the shape of a valid doc link:
  - If it's a local symbol (`ImportPath=="" && Recv==""`): raw text must be exactly a valid Go identifier (optionally with `*` prefix? The parser
trims `*`, but `[*Foo]` is valid in Go doc and means same as `[Foo]`). The task says "Ensure bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier
characters is not treated as a valid link." `*` is a non-identifier character but `[*Foo]` is valid doc link syntax. Hmm. The task might not consider
pointer syntax. But the comment parser explicitly supports `*` prefix (it trims it). Should we support `[*Foo]`? Go doc does. I'll support it by
allowing an optional `*` prefix when validating. Actually, if we reconstruct text and it starts with `*`, we can strip it before checking identifiers.
  - If it's a method (`ImportPath=="" && Recv!=""`): raw text must be `ident.ident` (two identifiers).
  - If it's a package link (`ImportPath!="" && Name==""`): raw text must be a valid import path (allows `/`, `-`, `.`, `_`, alphanumerics) OR a valid
identifier package name. Since the parser treats `[math]` as package link, we should allow single identifier. But `[foo bar]` contains a space →
reject. `[foo-bar]` contains `-` → should we reject? A single-element package name with `-` is not valid (package names are identifiers, no hyphens).
But import paths can have hyphens. However `[foo-bar]` alone (no slash, no dot symbol) — in Go doc, a hyphenated name isn't a standard package link. I
think rejecting is safe and matches "non-identifier characters".
  - If it's a qualified symbol (`ImportPath!="" && Name!=""`): raw text is `imp.ident` or `imp.ident.ident`. `imp` can be a package name (identifier)
or an import path. Need to allow import paths. But we should reject spaces. Also reject if imp part contains characters that are neither identifiers
nor valid import path chars.

Let me simplify: reconstruct raw text, then:
1. Strip a single leading `*`.
2. Split on "." — but import paths contain "/" not dots? Actually import paths CAN contain dots (e.g. `gopkg.in/yaml.v2`). But
`[gopkg.in/yaml.v2.Sym]` is allowed. Splitting on "." would break that. Hmm.

// Package analyzer implements `go/analysis` compatible interfaces.
package analyzer

import (
	"github.com/go-critic/go-critic/linter"

	"golang.org/x/tools/go/analysis"
)

// Analyzer exports go-critic checkers as analysis-compatible object.
// The set of enabled checkers is controlled via the flags.
// Per-checker params are also passed via the flags.
var Analyzer = &analysis.Analyzer{
	Name: "ruleguard",
	Doc:  "The most opinionated Go source code linter",
	Run:  runAnalyzer,
}

// DisableCache disables initialization optimization.
// This should only be useful for analyzer testing.
var DisableCache = false

var (
	flagGoVersion string
	flagEnable    string
	flagDisable   string
	flagEnableAll bool
	flagDebugInit bool
)

var (
	intParams    = make(map[string]*int)
	boolParams   = make(map[string]*bool)
	stringParams = make(map[string]*string)
)

var registeredCheckers = linter.GetCheckersInfo()

func init() {
	Analyzer.Flags.BoolVar(&flagDebugInit, "debug-init", false,
		`print go-critic initialization related debug info`)
	Analyzer.Flags.BoolVar(&flagEnableAll, "enable-all", false,
		`identical to -enable with all checkers listed. If true, -enable is ignored`)
	Analyzer.Flags.StringVar(&flagEnable, "enable", "#diagnostic,#style,#security",
		`comma-separated list of enabled checkers. Can include #tags`)
	Analyzer.Flags.StringVar(&flagDisable, "disable", "<default>",
		`comma-separated list of checkers to be disabled. Can include #tags`)
	Analyzer.Flags.StringVar(&flagGoVersion, "go", "",
		`select the Go version to target. Leave as string for the latest`)

	for _, info := range registeredCheckers {
		for pname, param := range info.Params {
			key := checkerParamName(info, pname)
			switch v := param.Value.(type) {
			case int:
				intParams[key] = Analyzer.Flags.Int(key, v, param.Usage)
			case bool:
				boolParams[key] = Analyzer.Flags.Bool(key, v, param.Usage)
			case string:
				stringParams[key] = Analyzer.Flags.String(key, v, param.Usage)
			default:
				panic("unreachable") // Checked in AddChecker
			}
		}
	}
}

func checkerParamName(info *linter.CheckerInfo, pname string) string {
	return "@" + info.Name + "." + pname
}

Baseline works. Now let me experiment with go/types and comment.Parser in a temp program to solidify the design. Let me write a program that uses the
actual repo's types.Info to resolve imports and test lookups. But first, let me understand how to get package scope for an imported package from
`*types.Package`. `pkg.Scope()` returns `*types.Scope` for the package; `scope.Lookup(name)` returns object.

For embedded field/method lookup:
- `types.NewMethodSet(typ)` gives all methods including promoted.
- For fields: recursively traverse struct fields. A helper:
```go
func lookupField(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
    switch t := typ.(type) {
    case *types.Named:
        return lookupField(t.Underlying(), name)
    case *types.Pointer:
        return lookupField(t.Elem(), name)
    case *types.Struct:
        for i := 0; i < t.NumFields(); i++ {
            f := t.Field(i)
            if f.Name() == name {
                return true
            }
            if f.Embedded() {
                if lookupField(f.Type(), name) {
                    return true
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return false
}
```
But this would also find unexported fields from other packages (embedded fields can't promote unexported names across packages, but within the same
package it's fine). For simplicity, this works for the checker.

Actually, there's a subtlety: `T.M` where `T` is a type and `M` is a field. The comment package's DocLink for `[T.M]` has Recv=T, Name=M. It doesn't
distinguish method vs field. So we need to check both.

`go/parser` to check `types.NewMethodSet` and struct field traversal.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"go/ast"
	"go/importer"
	"go/parser"
	"go/token"
	"go/types"
)

func main() {
	src := `package p

import "fmt"

type Base struct { X int }
func (Base) M() {}
type Embedded struct { Base }
func (Embedded) N() {}
type T struct { Embedded; Y int }
var v T
func F() { fmt.Println(v.X, v.M, v.Embedded, v.Base) }
`
	fset := token.NewFileSet()
	f, _ := parser.ParseFile(fset, "p.go", src, 0)
	conf := types.Config{Importer: importer.Default()}
	info := &types.Info{Types: map[ast.Expr]types.TypeAndValue{}, Defs: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}, Uses: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}}
	pkg, _ := conf.Check("p", fset, []*ast.File{f}, info)

	// builtins
	for _, n := range []string{"int", "string", "len", "Error", "error", "any", "comparable", "byte", "rune", "nil", "true"} {
		fmt.Printf("Universe.Lookup(%q) = %v\n", n, types.Universe.Lookup(n))
	}

	// find T
	obj := pkg.Scope().Lookup("T")
	tn := obj.(*types.TypeName)
	named := tn.Type().(*types.Named)
	fmt.Println("T methods:")
	ms := types.NewMethodSet(named)
	for i := 0; i < ms.Len(); i++ {
		fmt.Println("  ", ms.At(i).Obj().Name(), ms.At(i).Obj().Parent())
	}

	fmt.Println("T struct:")
	for _, u := range []types.Type{named, types.NewPointer(named)} {
		fmt.Printf("underlying of %T: %v\n", u, u.Underlying())
		st := u.Underlying().(*types.Struct)
		for i := 0; i < st.NumFields(); i++ {
			fld := st.Field(i)
			fmt.Printf("  field %s embedded=%v type=%v\n", fld.Name(), fld.Embedded(), fld.Type())
		}
	}
}

Actually, before going further, let me think about the astwalk walker design. I'll add:
- `doc_link_visitor.go` (or add to visitor.go)
- `doc_link_walker.go`

The `DocLinkVisitor`:
```go
type DocLinkVisitor interface {
    walkerEvents
    VisitDocLink(decl ast.Node, doc *ast.CommentGroup)
}
```

The walker:
```go
type docLinkWalker struct {
    visitor DocLinkVisitor
}

func (w *docLinkWalker) WalkFile(f *ast.File) {
    if !w.visitor.EnterFile(f) {
        return
    }
    w.visitDecls(f)
}
```

It should mirror docCommentWalker but pass the decl node. Let me write it:
```go
func (w *docLinkWalker) WalkFile(f *ast.File) {
    if !w.visitor.EnterFile(f) {
        return
    }
    for _, decl := range f.Decls {
        switch decl := decl.(type) {
        case *ast.FuncDecl:
            if decl.Doc != nil {
                w.visitor.VisitDocLink(decl, decl.Doc)
            }
        case *ast.GenDecl:
            if decl.Doc != nil {
                w.visitor.VisitDocLink(decl, decl.Doc)
            }
            for _, spec := range decl.Specs {
                switch spec := spec.(type) {
                case *ast.ImportSpec:
                    if spec.Doc != nil {
                        w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
                    }
                case *ast.ValueSpec:
                    if spec.Doc != nil {
                        w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
                    }
                case *ast.TypeSpec:
                    if spec.Doc != nil {
                        w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
                    }
                    ast.Inspect(spec.Type, func(n ast.Node) bool {
                        if n, ok := n.(*ast.Field); ok {
                            if n.Doc != nil {
                                w.visitor.VisitDocLink(n, n.Doc)
                            }
                        }
                        return true
                    })
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Wait, but the requirement says "Emit each diagnostic at the position of the documented declaration node". For a GenDecl.Doc that documents a `type T
struct{}`, if we pass the GenDecl, Pos is at `type`. If we pass the TypeSpec, Pos is at `T`. Both are on the same line, so tests don't care. But
"documented declaration node" — I think for a single type spec with GenDecl.Doc, the documented declaration is the GenDecl. For type group:
```go
type (
    // T docs
    T struct{}
)
```
the documented declaration is the TypeSpec. The walker handles both (GenDecl.Doc and TypeSpec.Doc), so we're good.

Now, the checker:

```go
package checkers

import (
    "go/ast"
    "go/doc/comment"
    "go/types"
    "strings"

    "github.com/go-critic/go-critic/checkers/internal/astwalk"
    "github.com/go-critic/go-critic/linter"
)

func init() {
    var info linter.CheckerInfo
    info.Name = "brokenDocLink"
    info.Tags = []string{linter.DiagnosticTag, linter.ExperimentalTag}
    info.Summary = "Detects broken doc comment links"
    info.Before = `// Foo does something.
// See [Bar] for details.
type Foo struct{}`
    info.After = `// Foo does something.
// See [Foo] for details.
type Foo struct{}`

    collection.AddChecker(&info, func(ctx *linter.CheckerContext) (linter.FileWalker, error) {
        c := &brokenDocLinkChecker{ctx: ctx}
        return astwalk.WalkerForDocLink(c), nil
    })
}

type brokenDocLinkChecker struct {
    astwalk.WalkHandler
    ctx *linter.CheckerContext

    file *ast.File
    // imports maps local package name -> imported package
    imports map[string]*types.Package
    // dotImports lists packages imported with dot
    dotImports []*types.Package
}

func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) EnterFile(f *ast.File) bool {
    c.file = f
    c.imports = make(map[string]*types.Package)
    c.dotImports = nil
    for _, spec := range f.Imports {
        pkg := importedPackage(c.ctx, spec)
        if pkg == nil { continue }
        if spec.Name != nil && spec.Name.Name == "." {
            c.dotImports = append(c.dotImports, pkg)
            continue
        }
        name := localName(c.ctx, spec)
        c.imports[name] = pkg
    }
    return true
}
```

Wait, but `ctx.PkgObjects` already provides a map from *types.PkgName -> name. But it resets per file via `SetFileInfo`. Since `SetFileInfo` is called
before `Check(f)`, and our EnterFile is called during `Check(f)`, we could just use `ctx.PkgObjects`. But PkgObjects only includes entries for all
imports and maps name. For dot imports, what name is recorded? `resolvePkgObjects`:
```go
if spec.Name != nil {
    obj := ctx.TypesInfo.ObjectOf(spec.Name)
    ctx.PkgObjects[obj.(*types.PkgName)] = spec.Name.Name
} else {
    obj := ctx.TypesInfo.Implicits[spec]
    ctx.PkgObjects[obj.(*types.PkgName)] = obj.Name()
}
```
For dot import, spec.Name.Name is ".", and obj.Name() — for a dot import, `PkgName.Name()` returns "."? Let me check. Actually, for dot imports,
`ctx.TypesInfo.ObjectOf(spec.Name)` where spec.Name is `.` (an *ast.Ident with Name="."). Hmm, does types.Info.ObjectOf handle that? Let me check. In

in Uses? I think for dot imports, the import spec's name `.` is not in Uses; instead `Implicits[spec]` holds the PkgName. Let me check the go/types
behavior. Actually I recall `TypesInfo.Implicits` records implicit objects for `import . "fmt"` (the PkgName). And `Uses` might not contain the `.`

package main

import (

func main() {
	src := `package p
import (
	"fmt"
	alias "strings"
	. "sort"
)
var _ = fmt.Sprintf
var _ = alias.ToUpper
var _ = Ints
`

	conf := types.Config{Importer: importer.Default()}
	info := &types.Info{Types: map[ast.Expr]types.TypeAndValue{}, Defs: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}, Uses: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}, Implicits: map[ast.Node]types.Object{}}
	pkg, err := conf.Check("p", fset, []*ast.File{f}, info)
	fmt.Println("err:", err, "pkg:", pkg)

		if spec.Name != nil {
			obj := info.ObjectOf(spec.Name)
			fmt.Printf("  ObjectOf(name): %T %v\n", obj, obj)
			if pn, ok := obj.(*types.PkgName); ok {
				fmt.Printf("  PkgName.Name=%q Imported=%v\n", pn.Name(), pn.Imported().Path())
			}
		}
		imp := info.Implicits[spec]
		fmt.Printf("  Implicits: %T %v\n", imp, imp)
		if pn, ok := imp.(*types.PkgName); ok {
			fmt.Printf("  PkgName.Name=%q Imported=%v\n", pn.Name(), pn.Imported().Path())
		}
	}
}

So:
- Package scope `pkg.Scope()` contains only the package's own top-level declarations (not imports, not dot-imported symbols).
- File scope `info.Scopes[f]` contains the file's imports (PkgName) and dot-imported symbols.

For local references `[X]`, we need to look up in the current package's declarations AND dot-imported symbols. So:
1. Check `ctx.Pkg.Scope().Lookup(X)` (own declarations).
2. Check each dot-imported package's scope for exported X.
3. Also maybe check `types.Universe` (builtins) but we skip those.

Actually wait — the requirement says "dot-imported symbols count as local". So for `[X]`, if X is exported from a dot-imported package, it's valid. So
we should check dot imports.

For qualified references `[pkg.X]`, we resolve pkg to an imported package (via file imports), then look up X in that package's scope
(`pkg.Scope().Lookup(X)`). Only exported symbols are accessible via qualified reference, so if X is unexported and not in the same package, it
wouldn't resolve. But `Lookup` would find unexported too. We might want to check `obj.Exported()`. But the task doesn't mention exportedness. Let's
keep simple: look up in scope; if found, valid.

Wait, but for a reference like `[pkg.x]` (lowercase x), the comment parser would treat `pkg.x` as... `splitDocName("pkg.x")`: i=3, name="x" not
capitalized → returns ("pkg.x","",false) → pkg="pkg.x" → lookupPkg("pkg.x"). With always-true, DocLink imp="pkg.x", name="". So `[pkg.x]` would be
treated as a package-only link with path "pkg.x" — and we'd likely skip it as invalid (contains dot in package path? Actually "pkg.x" could be a valid
import path like "gopkg.in/yaml"). Hmm. If someone writes `[fmt.println]` (typo, lowercase), Go doc wouldn't treat it as a symbol link; it'd be a
broken package link candidate. Our checker might report `package "fmt.println" is not imported`? That might be considered a false positive. But the
task says "Ensure bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is not treated as a valid link." `fmt.println` contains no spaces but
has a dot and lowercase. It's not a valid symbol link because symbol must be capitalized (Go doc link semantics). We should probably skip it, not
report "package not imported".

How do we distinguish `[math]` (valid package link) from `[foo bar]` (invalid) and `[fmt.println]` (probably invalid)?

The parser's behavior:
- `[math]` → imp="math", name="" (single lowercase identifier → package link candidate)
- `[fmt.println]` → imp="fmt.println", name="" (because last part after dot is lowercase, not a symbol)
- `[foo bar]` → imp="foo bar", name=""
- `[Foo.Bar]` → imp="", recv="Foo", name="Bar" (local method)
- `[pkg.Foo]` → imp="pkg", name="Foo"

So for name=="" (package-only links), the imp can be:
- A single identifier (e.g. "math", "fmt") → valid package name reference. Validate that imp is an identifier (no spaces, no hyphens).
- A path with dots but no slash (e.g. "fmt.println", "gopkg.in/yaml") → could be an import path. But a package-only doc link like `[gopkg.in/yaml]` is
valid (links to package docs). Hmm, but `[fmt.println]` also has dots; would it be a valid import path? "fmt.println" isn't a standard package, and
"gopkg.in/yaml" is. Without network, we can only check against the file's imports. So if "fmt.println" isn't imported, we'd report `package
"fmt.println" is not imported` — which might be a false positive for a typo'd symbol link. But arguably it IS a broken link either way. The
requirement about spaces/non-identifier chars suggests they specifically want to avoid flagging `[foo bar]` and `[foo-bar]`, but maybe `[fmt.println]`
should be flagged as package not imported. I think that's acceptable.

Actually, let me reconsider. Maybe the intended validation is purely structural:
- A valid link text must consist of Go identifiers separated by dots, OR be a package import path (which can contain dots, slashes, hyphens, etc.).
- "bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is not treated as a valid link" — so reject spaces and characters that are not valid
in identifiers/import paths. For `[foo bar]` → space → reject. For `[foo-bar]` → hyphen... but hyphen is valid in import paths. Hmm, but a
single-element name `foo-bar` is not a valid import path (no slash) and not an identifier, so reject. For `[Foo.Bar Baz]` → space → reject.

I think the cleanest validation:
1. Reconstruct raw text from `link.Text` (as written inside brackets).
2. Strip leading `*` if present.
3. If the text contains any whitespace → skip.
4. Determine if it's a package path or symbol reference:
   - If `link.Name == ""`: it's a package-only reference. Validate that the text is either a valid Go identifier OR a valid import path (contains "/"
or matches a real imported path). Actually to avoid false positives, let's require that the package reference resolves to an imported package. If it
does, valid. If not, report `package "X" is not imported` — but only if X is plausibly a package reference. To determine plausibility, require the
text to be a valid identifier (for single-element names like "math", "fmt") or a valid import path (for paths with slashes). Reject things like
"foo-bar", "fmt.println", "Foo.Bar Baz" (space). But `fmt.println` is a dot-containing single-element path without slash — is that "plausible"? It's
not a standard Go package path. I'd reject it as not a valid package reference (since it contains a dot and no slash, and isn't an identifier). That
avoids flagging `[fmt.println]` (lowercase symbol typos). This seems consistent with "non-identifier characters" — a dot in a single-element name is
not an identifier character for a package name.

Hmm, but import paths can be single-element like "math", "io" — those are identifiers. Multi-element like "gopkg.in/yaml" have both dots and slashes.
So a valid package path with no slash is just an identifier (single element) OR could be "rsc.io/quote" (has slash). A path with dots but no slash is
unusual (e.g., "gopkg.in/yaml" has slash). So rule: a package-only reference is valid if:
   - it's a valid Go identifier (e.g. "math", "fmt"), OR
   - it's a valid import path containing "/" (e.g. "example.com/foo").

Let me define `isValidPackagePath(s)`:
```go
func isValidPackageRef(s string) bool {
    if token.IsIdentifier(s) { return true }  // math, fmt
    return strings.Contains(s, "/") && validImportPath(s)
}
```
And `validImportPath` allows letters, digits, `-`, `_`, `.`, `/`, `~`? Let's keep it simple: all chars in letters/digits/`-`/`_`/`.`/`/`, and
non-empty.

For qualified references `[pkg.Sym]` or `[pkg.Recv.Method]`, the `imp` field (`pkg`) must be either a valid identifier (package name/alias) or a valid
import path containing "/". The `Name` is already a capitalized identifier, `Recv` is a capitalized identifier. So we validate `imp` the same way.

But wait — there's the case `[Foo.Bar]` which is a local method (imp="", recv="Foo", name="Bar"). Here raw text is `Foo.Bar`, two identifiers. Good.

And `[pkg.Recv.Method]` raw text: imp="pkg", recv="Recv", name="Method" — three identifiers. Good.

Let me now decide on how to reconstruct the raw text. `DocLink.Text` is `[]Text`, containing Plain/Italic. We can write:
```go
func docLinkText(l *comment.DocLink) string {
    var sb strings.Builder
    for _, t := range l.Text {
        switch v := t.(type) {
        case comment.Plain:
            sb.WriteString(string(v))
        case comment.Italic:
            sb.WriteString(string(v))
        case *comment.Link:
            // shouldn't happen inside DocLink
        }
    }
    return sb.String()
}
```
But `comment.Italic` is `type Italic string`, so `string(v)` works. `*comment.Link` has Text too, but unlikely inside DocLink.

Actually, there's a simpler approach: the fields (ImportPath, Recv, Name) already encode the structured link. We could validate:
- If Name != "" and Recv != "" and ImportPath != "": qualified method `[pkg.Recv.Method]`. Validate ImportPath is valid package ref, then look up.
- If Name != "" and Recv == "" and ImportPath != "": qualified symbol `[pkg.Sym]`.
- If Name != "" and Recv != "" and ImportPath == "": local method `[Recv.Method]`.
- If Name != "" and Recv == "" and ImportPath == "": local symbol `[Sym]`.
- If Name == "": package-only `[pkg]`.

But the raw text check is needed to reject `[foo bar]` (imp="foo bar"), `[foo-bar]` (imp="foo-bar"), `[Foo.Bar Baz]` (imp="Foo.Bar Baz"), and
`[pkg.lower.sym]` where the whole thing is imp="pkg.lower.sym", name="". For the last one, with Name=="" and imp containing dots, we'd reject unless
it's a valid import path with slash. Good.

I think reconstructing raw text and validating the "package part" is the cleanest.

Let me define:
```go
func isIdentifier(s string) bool {
    if s == "" { return false }
    for i, r := range s {
        if !(r == '_' || unicode.IsLetter(r) || (i > 0 && unicode.IsDigit(r))) {
            return false
        }
    }
    return true
}
```
Or use `token.IsIdentifier` from go/token. `token.IsIdentifier` exists in Go 1.13+. It uses `ast.IsExported`? Let me check. Actually
`token.IsIdentifier(name)` returns true if name is a valid identifier. Good, I'll use that.

For import paths:
```go
func isImportPath(s string) bool {
    if s == "" { return false }
    for _, r := range s {
        if !(unicode.IsLetter(r) || unicode.IsDigit(r) || r == '-' || r == '_' || r == '.' || r == '/' || r == '~') {
            return false
        }
    }
    return true
}
```
But for validation of a package reference, we need: if it's not an identifier, it must contain "/" AND be a valid import path. Because `fmt.println`
(no slash) would otherwise pass `isImportPath`. So:
```go
func isValidPackageRef(s string) bool {
    if token.IsIdentifier(s) { return true }
    return strings.Contains(s, "/") && isImportPath(s)
}
```

Now for local symbol/method refs, the imp is "" and no package validation needed. But we still should validate raw text doesn't have
spaces/non-identifiers. For `[Foo]`, parser already ensures name is capitalized identifier. For `[*Foo]`, parser trims `*`; raw text is `*Foo`, which
contains a non-identifier char but is valid doc link syntax. The requirement "Ensure bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is
not treated as a valid link" — does `*` count? `[*Foo]` is a valid link (points to Foo) and the parser intentionally supports it. I think we should
allow `*` prefix since it's part of Go doc link syntax. But to be safe, maybe the intended checker rejects it? Let me think... The comment parser's
docLink explicitly trims "*", meaning `[*Foo]` is intended to be a valid doc link. A checker that flags `[*Foo]` as broken would be wrong. I'll allow
leading `*`.

For local refs, we validate raw text:
```go
func isValidSymbolRef(text string) bool {
    text = strings.TrimPrefix(text, "*")
    if token.IsIdentifier(text) { return true }
    // or text matches ident.ident / ident.ident.ident
    parts := strings.Split(text, ".")
    if len(parts) >= 2 && len(parts) <= 3 {
        for _, p := range parts {
            if !token.IsIdentifier(p) { return false }
        }
        return true
    }
    return false
}
```
But for `[Foo.Bar Baz]` local? No, that has space → imp="Foo.Bar Baz" → not local. For `[Foo-Bar]` → splitDocName: i=-1? Actually `[Foo-Bar]`:
LastIndex(".") = -1, name="Foo-Bar", isName("Foo-Bar") false (hyphen not in ident) → returns ("Foo-Bar","",false) → pkg="Foo-Bar", lookupPkg called →
imp="Foo-Bar", name="". So it's a package-only link with invalid pkg → reject.

I'll implement validation based on the structured fields plus a raw-text check for the package part. Let me define a helper that validates and returns
the "package name/path" to use in messages and lookup:

```go
// Returns package ref (name or path) and whether it's a valid package ref.
func validPkgRef(s string) bool {
    if token.IsIdentifier(s) {
        return true
    }
    return strings.Contains(s, "/") && isImportPath(s)
}
```

Now let me think about resolving the package:
- For a package reference `[pkg]` or `[pkg.Sym]` where `pkg` is an identifier: look up `c.imports[pkg]` (map from local name to *types.Package). If
not found, report `package "pkg" is not imported`. For renamed imports, `c.imports["alias"]` = pkg; message uses "alias" as written. For dot imports,
there's no alias; if someone writes `[sort.Ints]` while `sort` is dot-imported, that's not valid because the name isn't available — report package not
imported. Good.
- If `pkg` contains "/" (import path): look up file imports by path. If found, use that package; else report `package "pkg" is not imported` (path as
written).

For local references, check:
1. `ctx.Pkg.Scope().Lookup(name)` — own declarations.
2. For each dot-imported package, `pkg.Scope().Lookup(name)` if exported.
3. If not found and `types.Universe.Lookup(name)` != nil → skip (builtin).
4. Else report `unknown symbol "X" in current package`.

Wait, but what about local method references `[Recv.Method]`? Here `Recv` is a type name in current package. We check:
1. Find `Recv` in current package scope. If not found, check dot imports. If not found and builtin → skip? Builtins that are types include `int`,
`string`, etc. — but those are lowercase and would be treated as package links, not local methods. A method ref `[int.String]`? Recv="int"? No, `int`
is lowercase so `[int.String]` → splitDocName("int.String"): i=3, name="String" cap, before="int"; splitDocName("int") → not cap → pkg="int", recv="";
so it's `[pkg.String]` where pkg="int", name="String", not a local method. So local method refs always have capitalized Recv. Builtin types with
capitalized names: `Error`? Not a builtin. So local method refs won't be builtins typically. But to be safe, if Recv is a builtin type (e.g., if
someone writes `[Any.Method]` where Any isn't a builtin... no). Let's not worry; but the requirement "References to Go builtins must not be flagged"
mainly applies to local symbol refs like `[int]`, `[len]`, and maybe `[Error]`.

Actually wait: `[error]` (lowercase) → package link candidate "error". `types.Universe.Lookup("error")` non-nil. We should skip. `[any]`,
`[comparable]`, `[byte]`, `[rune]` similarly. `[int]`, `[string]`, `[bool]`, etc. So in the package-only path, before reporting "package not
imported", check if the name is a predeclared identifier (`types.Universe.Lookup(name) != nil`) → skip.

What about `[len]`, `[cap]`, `[append]` — universe builtins → skip. `[nil]`, `[true]`, `[false]` → universe → skip. Good.

Now for qualified references `[pkg.Sym]`:
1. Resolve pkg. If not imported → `package "pkg" is not imported`.
2. Look up `Sym` in pkg.Scope(). If not found → `"Sym" not found in package "pkg"`.
   - Should we check `obj.Exported()`? If Sym is unexported and pkg is different, `[pkg.x]` wouldn't be a symbol link anyway (parser wouldn't set
Name). But `[pkg.X]` with X unexported in the same package? You can't qualify with the package name for same-package references. Hmm, if pkg ==
current package (imported itself? no), skip. Let's just check scope lookup.
3. If found → valid.

For qualified method refs `[pkg.Recv.Method]`:
1. Resolve pkg. If not imported → `package "pkg" is not imported`.
2. Find `Recv` in pkg.Scope(). If not found → `type "Recv" not found in package "pkg"`.

3. If found but not a type → `"F" is not a type` (F=Recv). Message format: `"F" is not a type`.
4. If found and is a type → check member `Method` exists (method or field, including embedded). If not → `type "T" has no method or field "M"`.

For local method refs `[Recv.Method]`:
1. Find `Recv` in current package scope (including dot imports? dot-imported types count as local symbols; if a dot-imported type has a method,
`[Recv.Method]` should work). So yes, check dot imports.
2. If not found → `type "Recv" not found in current package`.
3. If not a type → `"F" is not a type`.
4. If type → check member.

Now, what's a "type" in go/types? `*types.TypeName` (including alias type names? aliases are `*types.TypeName` with `IsAlias()`). We should accept any
`*types.TypeName` as a type. Also builtin types like `int` are `*types.TypeName` in universe.

Now member lookup. Given a `*types.Named` (or other type), check if a member (method or field) named M exists, including promoted/embedded:
- Methods: `types.NewMethodSet(typ).Lookup(pkg, M)` returns `*types.Selection` if found. But `NewMethodSet` needs a `*types.Package` for method
lookup? `ms.Lookup(pkg, name)` returns selection. Actually `(*types.MethodSet).Lookup(pkg, name)`. For unexported methods, pkg matters. For our
purposes, use `types.NewMethodSet(typ)` and iterate; simpler: check `ms.Lookup(nil, name)`? Let me check signature. In go/types, `func (s *MethodSet)
Lookup(pkg *types.Package, name string) *Selection`. If pkg is nil, it matches any? Let me test.
- Fields: walk struct fields recursively through embedded fields. Also, methods could be on the type itself. But `NewMethodSet` only covers methods,
not fields. So do both.

Let me write a helper:
```go
func hasMember(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
    if hasMethod(typ, name) { return true }
    return hasField(typ, name)
}

func hasMethod(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
    ms := types.NewMethodSet(typ)
    for i := 0; i < ms.Len(); i++ {
        if ms.At(i).Obj().Name() == name {
            return true
        }
    }
    return false
}

func hasField(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
    switch t := typ.(type) {
    case *types.Named:
        return hasField(t.Underlying(), name)
    case *types.Pointer:
        return hasField(t.Elem(), name)
    case *types.Struct:
        for i := 0; i < t.NumFields(); i++ {
            f := t.Field(i)
            if f.Name() == name {
                return true
            }
            if f.Embedded() && hasField(f.Type(), name) {
                return true
            }
        }
    }
    return false
}
```

But there's a subtlety: for a pointer to named type, `types.NewMethodSet(types.NewPointer(named))` includes methods, and `hasField` should dereference
pointers. For interfaces, `NewMethodSet` on interface gives its methods. For a type alias to a struct, underlying handles it. For a basic type like
`int`, no members.

Let me test this in the prototype with the embedded example, including `T.X` (field promoted), `T.M` (method promoted), `T.Embedded` (embedded field),
`T.Y` (direct field), `T.NoSuch`.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"go/ast"
	"go/importer"
	"go/parser"
	"go/token"
	"go/types"
)

func hasMethod(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
	ms := types.NewMethodSet(typ)
	for i := 0; i < ms.Len(); i++ {
		if ms.At(i).Obj().Name() == name {
			return true
		}
	}
	return false
}

func hasField(typ types.Type, name string) bool {
	switch t := typ.(type) {
	case *types.Named:
		return hasField(t.Underlying(), name)
	case *types.Pointer:
		return hasField(t.Elem(), name)
	case *types.Struct:
		for i := 0; i < t.NumFields(); i++ {
			f := t.Field(i)
			if f.Name() == name {
				return true
			}
			if f.Embedded() && hasField(f.Type(), name) {
				return true
			}
		}
	}
	return false
}

func main() {
	src := `package p

type Base struct { X int }
func (Base) M() {}
type Embedded struct { Base }
func (Embedded) N() {}
type T struct { Embedded; Y int }
var v T
`
	fset := token.NewFileSet()
	f, _ := parser.ParseFile(fset, "p.go", src, 0)
	conf := types.Config{Importer: importer.Default()}
	info := &types.Info{Types: map[ast.Expr]types.TypeAndValue{}, Defs: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}, Uses: map[*ast.Ident]types.Object{}, Implicits: map[ast.Node]types.Object{}}
	pkg, _ := conf.Check("p", fset, []*ast.File{f}, info)
	tn := pkg.Scope().Lookup("T").(*types.TypeName)
	named := tn.Type().(*types.Named)
	for _, name := range []string{"X", "M", "N", "Y", "Embedded", "Base", "NoSuch"} {
		fmt.Printf("%s: method=%v field=%v\n", name, hasMethod(named, name), hasField(named, name))
	}
	// method set lookup with nil pkg
	ms := types.NewMethodSet(named)
	fmt.Println("lookup M with nil pkg:", ms.Lookup(nil, "M"))
}

Now let me think about the `comment.Parser` integration. I'll set:
- `LookupPackage: func(name string) (string, bool) { return name, true }` — accepts all package candidates.
- `LookupSym: func(recv, name string) bool { return true }` — accepts all local symbol/method candidates.

Then walk `doc.Content` recursively to collect `*comment.DocLink`. Need to handle Paragraph, List, Heading, Code (code blocks are plain text, no
links). The parser produces DocLink inside Paragraph.Text and List items' Paragraph.Text, and Heading has Text []Text (also could contain DocLink?
Headings can contain doc links). Let me write a recursive walker over blocks and texts.

Actually, I wonder if there's a simpler way: since `comment.DocLink` implements `comment.Text`, we can recursively walk all `Block` and `Text` nodes.
Let me implement a generic walker:

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) visitDoc(doc *comment.Doc) {
    for _, b := range doc.Content {
        c.visitBlock(b)
    }
}

func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) visitBlock(b comment.Block) {
    switch b := b.(type) {
    case *comment.Paragraph:
        c.visitTexts(b.Text)
    case *comment.List:
        for _, item := range b.Items {
            for _, content := range item.Content {
                c.visitBlock(content)
            }
        }
    case *comment.Heading:
        c.visitTexts(b.Text)
    case *comment.Code:
        // no links
    }
}

func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) visitTexts(ts []comment.Text) {
    for _, t := range ts {
        switch t := t.(type) {
        case *comment.DocLink:
            c.checkDocLink(t)
        case *comment.Link:
            // nested link inside? could have Text
            // skip
        }
    }
}
```

Note: `comment.Heading` has `Text []Text`, `comment.Paragraph` has `Text []Text`. `comment.List` has `Items []*ListItem`, each with `Content []Block`.
`comment.Code` has `Text string`. Also `comment.Link` has `Text []Text` but it's a URL link; not relevant.

Now the raw text extraction. `DocLink.Text` is the original bracket content. Let me write:
```go
func rawDocLinkText(l *comment.DocLink) string {
    var sb strings.Builder
    for _, t := range l.Text {
        switch t := t.(type) {
        case comment.Plain:
            sb.WriteString(string(t))
        case comment.Italic:
            sb.WriteString(string(t))
        }
    }
    return sb.String()
}
```

Wait, but `l.Text` may be `nil`? The parser sets it via `link.Text = d.parseText(nil, text[start+1:i], false)`. So it's populated. Good.

Now validation logic. Let me write a detailed plan for `checkDocLink`:

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) checkDocLink(decl ast.Node, l *comment.DocLink) {
    text := rawDocLinkText(l)
    if !isValidDocLinkText(text) {
        return
    }
    switch {
    case l.ImportPath != "" && l.Name == "":
        // package-only link [pkg]
        c.checkPackageRef(decl, text, l.ImportPath)
    case l.ImportPath != "" && l.Name != "" && l.Recv == "":
        // qualified symbol [pkg.Sym]
        c.checkQualifiedRef(decl, text, l.ImportPath, l.Name)
    case l.ImportPath != "" && l.Recv != "":
        // qualified method [pkg.Recv.Method]
        c.checkQualifiedMethod(decl, text, l.ImportPath, l.Recv, l.Name)
    case l.ImportPath == "" && l.Recv == "":
        // local symbol [Sym]
        c.checkLocalSymbol(decl, text, l.Name)
    case l.ImportPath == "" && l.Recv != "":
        // local method [Recv.Method]
        c.checkLocalMethod(decl, text, l.Recv, l.Name)
    }
}
```

But wait: what about `[pkg]` where `l.ImportPath` is "foo bar" — `isValidDocLinkText` should reject it before we get here. Let me define
`isValidDocLinkText` to validate based on raw text and fields:

Actually, the raw text check can be unified: the raw text must be a valid link text, which means:
- It is a Go identifier (possibly with leading `*`).
- Or it is identifier.identifier (method/symbol).
- Or it is identifier.identifier.identifier (qualified method).
- Or it is a package import path (with `/`) optionally followed by .identifier or .identifier.identifier.

But since `comment.Parser` already parses the structured fields, we can just validate the "package part" when it's non-empty. Let me think about which
invalid texts slip through with Name != "":
- `[foo bar.X]` → imp="foo bar", name="X" → imp contains space → reject.
- `[foo-bar.X]` → imp="foo-bar", name="X" → imp is not identifier, no slash → reject.
- `[Foo.Bar.Baz Qux]` → splitDocName: last dot, name="Baz Qux" not cap → whole thing imp="Foo.Bar.Baz Qux", name="" → package-only with space → reject
via package ref validation.
- `[Foo.Bar-Baz]` → name="Bar-Baz" not cap → imp="Foo.Bar-Baz" → package-only, no slash, not identifier → reject.
- `[*Foo]` → Name="Foo", ImportPath="", valid (we allow *).
- `[Foo..Bar]`? splitDocName: i=4, name="Bar" cap, before="Foo."; splitDocName("Foo.") → name="" not cap → pkg="Foo.", recv=""; lookupPkg("Foo.") →
imp="Foo.", name="Bar". Raw text has ".." — imp="Foo." not identifier, no slash → reject. Good.
- `[pkg.Sym.]`? name after last dot = "" not cap → imp="pkg.Sym." → package-only, not identifier, no slash → reject.
- `[pkg.Recv.Method]` valid.
- `[pkg.Recv.method]` → last name "method" not cap → imp="pkg.Recv.method" → package-only, no slash, not identifier → reject. Good — a lowercase
method typo isn't reported.

So the key validation is: whenever `l.ImportPath != ""`, validate `l.ImportPath` with `isValidPackageRef`. Whenever `l.Recv != ""` (local method),
validate `l.Recv` is an identifier (it's already capitalized, but e.g. `[*Foo.Bar]`? Raw text `*Foo.Bar` — parser trims `*`, then
splitDocName("Foo.Bar") → recv="Foo", name="Bar". So `[*Foo.Bar]` is treated as local method `Foo.Bar`; valid? Go doc allows `[*T.M]`? Actually the
`*` only applies to the first element, so `[*T.M]` means method M on type T (pointer receiver syntax). I think it's valid. We can allow it.)

Also, for local symbol `[Name]`, `l.Name` is guaranteed capitalized identifier by parser. So no extra validation needed beyond package-part checks.

But the requirement specifically says "Ensure bracket content containing spaces or non-identifier characters is not treated as a valid link." This
could also mean: if the raw text has a space, skip. E.g., `[Foo bar]` → name="Foo bar"? Let's see: splitDocName("Foo bar"): i=-1, name="Foo bar" not
cap → imp="Foo bar" → package-only, space → reject. Good. `[Foo Bar.Baz]`? name after last dot="Baz" cap, before="Foo Bar"; splitDocName("Foo Bar") →
not cap → imp="Foo Bar" → reject. Good.

So validating `l.ImportPath` with `isValidPackageRef` covers all cases where spaces/non-identifiers appear. And for local refs, parser already ensures
identifiers. But to be extra safe, I'll also validate raw text with a general check that it doesn't contain spaces (except... no spaces should ever be
in a valid link). Let me add: if `strings.ContainsAny(rawText, " \t\n")` → skip. That covers everything robustly.

Now, let me handle package resolution:

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) resolvePackage(ref string) *types.Package {
    // ref is either local package name/alias or full import path
    if pkg, ok := c.imports[ref]; ok {
        return pkg
    }
    for _, spec := range c.file.Imports {
        path, _ := strconv.Unquote(spec.Path.Value)
        if path == ref {
            return importedPkg(spec)
        }
    }
    return nil
}
```

Where `c.imports` maps local name -> *types.Package, built in EnterFile. Let me write a helper to get the imported package from an import spec:
```go
func importedPkg(ctx *linter.CheckerContext, spec *ast.ImportSpec) *types.Package {
    var obj types.Object
    if spec.Name != nil {
        obj = ctx.TypesInfo.ObjectOf(spec.Name)
    } else {
        obj = ctx.TypesInfo.Implicits[spec]
    }
    if pn, ok := obj.(*types.PkgName); ok {
        return pn.Imported()
    }
    return nil
}
```

And local name:
```go
func localPkgName(ctx, spec) string {
    if spec.Name != nil {
        return spec.Name.Name  // includes "." for dot imports
    }
    obj := ctx.TypesInfo.Implicits[spec].(*types.PkgName)
    return obj.Name() // package's actual name
}
```

Actually for default import, `obj.Name()` is the package's declared name (e.g. "fmt"). For a package whose name differs from path's last element (e.g.
`import "github.com/foo/bar"` where package is `bar`), the local name is `bar`, not the path. Good.

For dot imports, local name is "." — we don't add to `c.imports` map (or we add with key "."). We collect them in `c.dotImports`.

Now the local symbol check:
```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) checkLocalSymbol(decl ast.Node, ref, name string) {
    if c.lookupLocal(name) != nil {
        return
    }
    if types.Universe.Lookup(name) != nil {
        return // builtin
    }
    c.ctx.Warn(decl, `unknown symbol %q in current package`, ref)
}
```
Message format: `unknown symbol "X" in current package`. Note the format uses `%q` which yields `"X"`. The `<ref>` is the link text as written, e.g.

Now let me also reconsider the `isValidDocLinkText` function. I'll implement:

```go
func isDocLinkTextValid(raw string) bool {
    if strings.ContainsAny(raw, " \t\n") {
        return false
    }
    raw = strings.TrimPrefix(raw, "*")
    if raw == "" {
        return false
    }
    parts := strings.Split(raw, ".")
    switch len(parts) {
    case 1:
        return token.IsIdentifier(parts[0]) // local symbol or package name
    case 2, 3:
        // each part must be a Go identifier, except first may be an import path
        for i, p := range parts {
            if i == 0 {
                if !isValidPackageRef(p) {
                    return false
                }
            } else {
                if !token.IsIdentifier(p) {
                    return false
                }
            }
        }
        return true
    default:
        // import paths can contain dots and slashes, e.g. gopkg.in/yaml.v2.Sym
        // but len > 3 after splitting on "."... this breaks import paths
        return false
    }
}
```

Hmm, the len-based approach breaks for import paths with dots. Let me not over-engineer. Since the parser gives us structured fields, I'll validate
based on fields:

- If `l.ImportPath != ""`: require `isValidPackageRef(l.ImportPath)`.
- If `l.Recv != ""`: require `token.IsIdentifier(l.Recv)` (it's already capitalized, but double-check).
- If `l.Name != ""`: require `token.IsIdentifier(l.Name)` (parser guarantees).
- Plus raw text must not contain whitespace.

And `isValidPackageRef`:
```go
func isValidPackageRef(s string) bool {
    if token.IsIdentifier(s) {
        return true
    }
    return strings.Contains(s, "/") && isImportPath(s)
}
```

Wait, but for `[pkg.Recv.Method]`, `l.ImportPath` = "pkg" (an identifier), so it passes. For `[example.com/foo.Sym]`, `l.ImportPath` =
"example.com/foo" (contains "/" and is a valid path) → passes. For `[foo bar]`, `l.ImportPath`="foo bar" — has space → `token.IsIdentifier` false,
`isImportPath` checks chars and would reject space → false. Good. For `[foo-bar]`, `l.ImportPath`="foo-bar" — `token.IsIdentifier` false (hyphen), no
"/" → false. Good. For `[fmt.println]`, `l.ImportPath`="fmt.println" — not identifier (dot), no "/" → false. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: `l.ImportPath` for a package-only link `[math]` is "math" (identifier) → passes. For `[io.Reader]`, `l.ImportPath`="io"
(identifier) → passes. Good.

`l.Name`="Bar". Raw text contains `*` but no whitespace. We allow leading `*`. Good.

Now, for the `isImportPath` check, I need to be careful: `token.IsIdentifier` may return true for unicode identifiers. Import paths can contain
unicode too? Mostly ASCII. Let me write a permissive check:
```go
func isImportPath(s string) bool {
    if s == "" { return false }
    for _, r := range s {
        if unicode.IsLetter(r) || unicode.IsDigit(r) {
            continue
        }
        switch r {
        case '-', '_', '.', '/', '~':
            continue
        }
        return false
    }
    return true
}
```
But note `isValidPackageRef` already requires `token.IsIdentifier` OR contains "/" + isImportPath. So `gopkg.in/yaml` (contains "/") passes
isImportPath. `rsc.io/quote` passes. Good.

Now, let me think about whether to use `types.Universe` to skip builtins in all paths. For package-only links like `[int]`, `l.ImportPath`="int". If
"int" isn't imported, we'd normally report `package "int" is not imported`. But `int` is a builtin, so skip. For `[string]`, same. For `[len]`,
`[cap]`, same. For `[error]`, `[any]`, `[comparable]`, same. For `[byte]`, `[rune]`, same. For `[nil]`, `[true]`, `[false]`, same. So in
`checkPackageRef`, before reporting, check `types.Universe.Lookup(ref) != nil` → skip.

But wait — what if a user actually imports a package named "int" (impossible, int is predeclared and can't be a package name? You can have `import int
"fmt"`? Actually you can't name a package "int" because it's a predeclared identifier? Let me check — Go allows importing with alias `int`?
Predeclared identifiers can be shadowed, so `import int "fmt"` is probably allowed. If so, `[int.Sprint]` would be valid via the alias. But our
`types.Universe` skip is only in the "not found" path, so if the package is imported we'd resolve it first and not skip. Good.)

For local symbols, `[Error]` — if not found in current package and `types.Universe.Lookup("Error")` is nil, report. If found as builtin (e.g. `[any]`

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) lookupLocal(name string) types.Object {
    if obj := c.ctx.Pkg.Scope().Lookup(name); obj != nil {
        return obj
    }
    for _, pkg := range c.dotImports {
        if obj := pkg.Scope().Lookup(name); obj != nil && obj.Exported() {
            return obj
        }
    }
    return nil
}
```

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) checkLocalMethod(decl ast.Node, ref, recv, name string) {
    obj := c.lookupLocal(recv)
    if obj == nil {
        if types.Universe.Lookup(recv) != nil { return }
        c.ctx.Warn(decl, `[%s]: type %q not found in current package`, ref, recv)
        return
    }
    tn, ok := obj.(*types.TypeName)
    if !ok {
        c.ctx.Warn(decl, `[%s]: %q is not a type`, ref, recv)
        return
    }
    if !hasMember(tn.Type(), name) {
        c.ctx.Warn(decl, `[%s]: type %q has no method or field %q`, ref, recv, name)
    }
}
```

```go
func (c *brokenDocLinkChecker) checkQualifiedMethod(decl ast.Node, ref, pkgRef, recv, name string) {
    pkg := c.resolvePackage(pkgRef)
    if pkg == nil {
        c.ctx.Warn(decl, `[%s]: package %q is not imported`, ref, pkgRef)

}
```

===== manifest =====
# Project manifest

This document exists to make it clear what fits `go-critic` project and what is not.<br>
In a sense, this manifest describes project philosophy.

This document may change over time.<br>
Contributors and maintainers are encouraged to discuss it and propose changes.<br>

## What go-critic is

It's hard to classify a particular linter due to similarities between them.

There are at least 4 linters-related categories:

1. Ones that you run frequently: on your CI, from your editor or IDE.
   They usually have a false positive rate that is close to zero.
   Warnings they give are not advices, but directives.
   [staticcheck](https://github.com/dominikh/go-tools) is a good example.
2. Rule-based checkers. An example of these is project-local codestyle checkers.
   They are highly customizable and usually check for local conventions
   as opposed to generally accepted guidelines.
   [checkstyle](https://github.com/qiniu/checkstyle) may be close to this category.
3. Linter runners. They typically don't implement checks by themselves, but are
   able to run other linters and aggregate their output.
   Examples: [gometalinter](https://github.com/alecthomas/gometalinter) and [golangci-lint](https://github.com/golangci/golangci-lint).
4. Linters that are made for code audit. Ones that help you to do code review,
   3rd-party library code quality evaluation and so on.
   False positive rate is quite high to make them worthwhile inside CI.
   You don't want to break a build due to the warnings they produce.
   There are advantages of this, see below.
   Example: [go-critic](https://github.com/go-critic/go-critic).

The advantages of not being bound to CI and close-to-zero false positive rate requirement is freedom.
We can find code that looks like a bug or suspicious.
Some checks can't be done without false positives, but they are still useful.
You *have* to check most `go-critic` warnings manually, this is why `quickfix`-like features doesn't
play well with `go-critic`. You may not want to fix issues found by `go-critic` automatically.

	"strings"
	"text/template"

	"github.com/go-critic/go-critic/checkers"
	"github.com/go-critic/go-critic/linter"
)

const (
	docsPath      = "../../docs/"
	templatesPath = docsPath + "templates/"
)

func main() {
	if err := checkers.InitEmbeddedRules(); err != nil {
		panic(err)
	}

	tmpl := parseTemplate(
		"overview.md.tmpl",
		"checker.partial.tmpl",
		"checker_tr.partial.tmpl")

	buf := bytes.Buffer{}
	err := tmpl.ExecuteTemplate(&buf, "overview", struct {
		Checkers []*linter.CheckerInfo
	}{
		Checkers: linter.GetCheckersInfo(),
	})
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatalf("render template: %v", err)
	}

	// A bit hacky but works, see https://github.com/go-critic/go-critic/pull/1403.
	buff := bytes.ReplaceAll(buf.Bytes(), []byte("<all>"), []byte("&lt;all&gt;"))

	if err := os.WriteFile(docsPath+"overview.md", buff, 0o600); err != nil {
		log.Fatalf("write output file: %v", err)
	}
}

func parseTemplate(names ...string) *template.Template {
	paths := make([]string, len(names))
	for i := range names {
		paths[i] = templatesPath + names[i]
	}
	funcMap := template.FuncMap{
		"ToLower": strings.ToLower,
	}
	return template.Must(template.New("overview").Funcs(funcMap).ParseFiles(paths...))
}

cd /app && cat > checkers/internal/astwalk/doc_link_walker.go <<'EOF'
package astwalk

import (
	"go/ast"
)

type docLinkWalker struct {
	visitor DocLinkVisitor
}

func (w *docLinkWalker) WalkFile(f *ast.File) {
	if !w.visitor.EnterFile(f) {
		return
	}

	for _, decl := range f.Decls {
		switch decl := decl.(type) {
		case *ast.FuncDecl:
			if decl.Doc != nil {
				w.visitor.VisitDocLink(decl, decl.Doc)
			}
		case *ast.GenDecl:
			if decl.Doc != nil {
				w.visitor.VisitDocLink(decl, decl.Doc)
			}
			for _, spec := range decl.Specs {
				switch spec := spec.(type) {
				case *ast.ImportSpec:
					if spec.Doc != nil {
						w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
					}
				case *ast.ValueSpec:
					if spec.Doc != nil {
						w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
					}
				case *ast.TypeSpec:
					if spec.Doc != nil {
						w.visitor.VisitDocLink(spec, spec.Doc)
					}
					ast.Inspect(spec.Type, func(n ast.Node) bool {
						if n, ok := n.(*ast.Field); ok {
							if n.Doc != nil {
								w.visitor.VisitDocLink(n, n.Doc)
							}
						}
						return true
					})
				}
			}
		}
	}
}
EOF

package astwalk

import (
	"go/ast"
)

// DocCommentVisitor visits every doc-comment.
// Does not visit doc-comments for function-local definitions (types, etc).
// Also does not visit package doc-comment (file-level doc-comments).
type DocCommentVisitor interface {
	VisitDocComment(*ast.CommentGroup)
}

// DocLinkVisitor visits every doc-comment along with the declaration
// that the comment documents.
type DocLinkVisitor interface {
	walkerEvents
	VisitDocLink(ast.Node, *ast.CommentGroup)
}

// FuncDeclVisitor visits every top-level function declaration.
type FuncDeclVisitor interface {
	walkerEvents
	VisitFuncDecl(*ast.FuncDecl)
}

// ExprVisitor visits every expression inside AST file.
type ExprVisitor interface {
	walkerEvents
	VisitExpr(ast.Expr)
}

// LocalExprVisitor visits every expression inside function body.
type LocalExprVisitor interface {
	walkerEvents
	VisitLocalExpr(ast.Expr)
}

// StmtListVisitor visits every statement list inside function body.
// This includes block statement bodies as well as implicit blocks

CALL s42_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_ZPquHqDLBzn93GjvzYTm9622
